\displaystyle-\frac{(r_{1}/r_{2})^{d/2}\sin\mbox{$\frac{\,{\pi d}\,}{2}$}}{4\pi^{d/2}\Gamma\big(\tfrac{d}{2}\big)\sin^{2}(\pi\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\displaystyle\frac{\,{\big[(1+n)_{\frac{d}{2}-1}\big]^{2}(1+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+n)_{\frac{d}{2}-1}(1-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+n)_{\frac{d}{2}-1}}\,}{(1+2n)_{d-1}}

\displaystyle\times(\mbox{$\frac{\,{d}\,}{2}$}+2n)\mathcal{C}_{d/2+2n,d}^{p_{1}p_{2}}\mathbf{F}_{d/2+2n,d}^{p_{1}}(r_{1})\mathbf{F}_{-d/2-2n,d}^{p_{2}}(r_{2})(r_{1}/r_{2})^{2n}.

(54)

Set 2C: background and local logarithmic tail.

The poles of Set 2C are from the
\csc^{2}(\pi\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{\prime})
factor in
\mathcal{I}_{\text{L},\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{p_{1}p_{2}}
. Similar to the poles of Set 1C in the last subsection, the
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{\prime}=0
pole is a simple pole, together with the
(r_{1}/r_{2})^{-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{\prime}}
term, it contributes the following result:

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{(\text{2C}0)}=

\displaystyle~\frac{\rho_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{\text{dS}}(0)}{8\pi^{2}}\Big\{\cos\frac{\pi\bar{p}_{12}}{2}+\cos\Big[\pi\Big(\frac{p_{12}+d}{2}\Big)\Big]\Big\}\mathbf{F}_{0,d}^{p_{1}}(r_{1})\mathbf{F}_{0,d}^{p_{2}}(r_{2}).

(55)

As has been mentioned in the last subsection,
\mathcal{J}_{\text{(2C0)}}
is canceled by
\mathcal{J}_{\text{(1C0)}}
in (
42
), so these two terms do not appear in the final result. On the other hand, the poles at
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{\prime}=\pm\mathrm{i}n
with
n\neq 0
are second-order poles. Their contributions to the integral can be found to be:

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{(\text{2C})}=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{8\pi^{2}}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{Z}_{n,d}^{p_{1}p_{2}}\Big\{\Pi_{n,d}(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)\Big[\log\displaystyle\frac{\,{r_{1}}\,}{r_{2}}+\mathbf{G}_{n,d}^{p_{1}}(r_{1})-\mathbf{G}_{-n,d}^{p_{2}}(r_{2})-\mathcal{Y}_{n,d}^{p_{1}p_{2}}+\mbox{$\frac{\,{1}\,}{n}$}\Big]-\Xi_{n,d}(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)\Big\}

\displaystyle\times\mathbf{F}_{n,d}^{p_{1}}(r_{1})\mathbf{F}_{-n,d}^{p_{2}}(r_{2})\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{r_{1}}\,}{r_{2}}\Big)^{n}.

(56)

The various quantities in this expression have been defined in (
44
)-(
48
). Similar to the result of Sec 1C poles, we find a term
\propto\log(r_{1}/r_{2})
which is nonanalytic in
r_{1}/r_{2}
when
r_{1}/r_{2}\rightarrow 0
. We call it the local logarithmic tail, as it is from the spectral integral of the local scalar seed integral. Again, all terms in
\mathcal{J}_{(\text{2C})}
except the one proportional to
\Xi_{n,d}(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)
vanish when
d=3
.

Set 2D: background.

Finally, there are poles arising from the Euler
\Gamma
factors in the dressed hypergeometric function in
\mathbf{F}_{\mp\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{\prime},d}^{p_{2}}(r_{2})
. See (
30
). These are simple poles that have no counterparts in the integrand of
\mathcal{J}_{(1)}
. The contribution from these poles is:

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{\text{(2D)}}=

\displaystyle~(2r_{1})^{d/2+1+p_{2}}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{n}2^{1+n}\sqrt{\pi}\big(\frac{d}{2}+p_{2}+1+n\big)}{n!}\Pi_{d/2+p_{2}+1+n,d}(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)

\displaystyle\times\mathcal{C}_{d/2+p_{2}+1+n,d}^{p_{1}p_{2}}\mathbf{F}_{d/2+p_{2}+1+n,d}^{p_{1}}(r_{1}){\,}_{2}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mathrm{F}\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1}\left[\begin{matrix}-\tfrac{n}{2},\tfrac{1-n}{2}\\
-\tfrac{d}{2}-p_{2}-n\end{matrix}\middle|r_{2}^{2}\right]\Big(\frac{r_{1}}{r_{2}}\Big)^{n}.

(57)

3.4
Contributions from the background tree integral

Finally, we consider the integral (
36
). Unlike all the previous integrals, in (
36
), the powers of
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
are independent of the integral variable
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{\prime}
, and thus we cannot use the power of
r_{1,2}
to decide on which side of the complex
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{\prime}
-plane to close the integral contour. On the other hand, as we show in App.
C.2
, the spectral function
\rho_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{\text{dS}}(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{\prime})
behaves for large
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{\prime}
like
|\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{\prime}|^{d-3}
, while the background tree seed integral
\mathcal{I}_{\text{BG},\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{\prime}}^{p_{1}p_{2}}
behaves like
|\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{\prime}|^{-2}
. So, the integrand of (
36
) dies away for large
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{\prime}
like
|\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{\prime}|^{d-4}
, and we can close the contour from either the upper-half or lower-half plane, so long as
d<3
.

It turns out simpler to close the contour from the lower-half
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{\prime}
plane, where the poles are entirely from the background tree seed integral (
31
) through the Pochhammer symbol
(\tfrac{\ell-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{\prime}+p_{2}+1}{2}+\tfrac{d}{4})_{m+1}
in the denominator. We assume that the spectral integral commutes with the summations over
m
and
\ell
. Then, for each fixed
m
and
\ell
, the poles of integrand of
\mathcal{J}_{(3)}
are:

Set 3:

\displaystyle\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{\prime}=-2\mathrm{i}n-\mathrm{i}\big(\mbox{$\frac{\,{1}\,}{2}$}d+p_{2}+1+\ell\big),~~~(n=0,1,\cdots,m)

(58)

Summing the residues at these poles, we get the following result:

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{\text{(3)}}=

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell,m=0}^{\infty}\sum_{n=0}^{m}\frac{(-1)^{\ell+n}\sin[\frac{\pi}{2}(p_{12}+d)](\ell+1)_{2m+d+p_{1}+p_{2}+1}}{2^{2m}n!(m-n)!\big(p_{2}+1+\mbox{$\frac{\,{d}\,}{2}$}+\ell+n\big)_{m+1}}\big(\mbox{$\frac{\,{1}\,}{2}$}d+p_{2}+1+\ell+2n\big)

\displaystyle\times\rho_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{\text{dS}}(-\mbox{$\frac{\,{\mathrm{i}d}\,}{2}$}-\mathrm{i}p_{2}-\mathrm{i}-\mathrm{i}\ell-2\mathrm{i}n)r_{1}^{2m+d+p_{12}+2}\Big(\frac{r_{1}}{r_{2}}\Big)^{\ell}.

(59)

3.5
Final result

At this point we have finished the computation of the loop seed integral by working out all three contributions in (
34
), (
35
), and (
36
). The final result for the loop seed integral
\mathcal{J}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{p_{1}p_{2}}(r_{1},r_{2})
in (
17
) can thus be found by summing up all contributions obtained from the previous three subsections, including (
3.2
), (
3.2
), (
3.2
), (
3.3
), (
3.3
), (
3.3
), (
3.3
), and (
3.4
). We find it helpful to regroup the many terms in the final result in terms of their analytic properties in the squeezed limit
r_{1,2}\rightarrow 0
. From the explicit results in the previous three subsections, we can identify four types of terms with distinct analytic properties in the squeezed limit, including a
nonlocal signal
piece
\mathcal{J}_{\text{NS}}^{p_{1}p_{2}}(r_{1},r_{2})
, which is proportional to
(r_{1}r_{2})^{\pm 2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}
in the squeezed limit; a
local signal
piece
\mathcal{J}_{\text{LS}}^{p_{1}p_{2}}(r_{1},r_{2})
, proportional to
(r_{1}/r_{2})^{\pm 2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}
; a
logarithmic tail
piece
\mathcal{J}_{\text{LT}}^{p_{1}p_{2}}(r_{1},r_{2})
, proportional to
\log r_{2}
; and, finally, a
background
piece
\mathcal{J}_{\text{BG}}^{p_{1}p_{2}}(r_{1},r_{2})
, which is analytic in the squeezed limit. In summary, we have:

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{p_{1}p_{2}}(r_{1},r_{2})=\mathcal{J}_{\text{NS}}^{p_{1}p_{2}}(r_{1},r_{2})+\mathcal{J}_{\text{LS}}^{p_{1}p_{2}}(r_{1},r_{2})+\mathcal{J}_{\text{LT}}^{p_{1}p_{2}}(r_{1},r_{2})+\mathcal{J}_{\text{BG}}^{p_{1}p_{2}}(r_{1},r_{2}).

(60)

Below we give the explicit expressions for the four pieces defined here. Similar to the expressions for the tree seed integral, the expressions below apply to the case of
r_{1}<r_{2}
. The result for
r_{1}>r_{2}
can be found from the following expressions by switching the variables
r_{1},p_{1}\leftrightarrow r_{2},p_{2}
.

Nonlocal signal.

The nonlocal signal
\mathcal{J}_{\text{NS}}^{p_{1}p_{2}}(r_{1},r_{2})
comes totally from the spectral integral (
34
) over the nonlocal tree seed integral through the Set-1A poles. See (
37
) and (
3.2
). The nonlocal signal features a pair of terms proportional to
(r_{1}r_{2})^{\pm 2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}
in the squeezed limit
r_{1,2}\rightarrow 0
. The full expression is:

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{\text{NS}}^{p_{1}p_{2}}=

\displaystyle-\frac{(r_{1}r_{2})^{d/2+2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}\sin[\pi(\mbox{$\frac{\,{d}\,}{2}$}+2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)]}{8\pi^{d/2}\Gamma\big(\tfrac{d}{2}\big)\sin^{2}(\pi\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\displaystyle\frac{\,{(1+n)_{\frac{d}{2}-1}\big[(1+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+n)_{\frac{d}{2}-1}\big]^{2}(1+2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+n)_{\frac{d}{2}-1}}\,}{(1+2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+2n)_{d-1}}

\displaystyle~\times(\mbox{$\frac{\,{d}\,}{2}$}+2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+2n)\mathcal{C}_{2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+d/2+2n,d}^{p_{1}p_{2}}\mathbf{F}_{2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+d/2+2n,d}^{p_{1}}(r_{1})\mathbf{F}_{2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+d/2+2n,d}^{p_{2}}(r_{2})(r_{1}r_{2})^{2n}+\text{c.c.}.

(61)

Here the coefficient
\mathcal{C}
is defined in (
32
) and the function
\mathbf{F}
is defined in (
33
). For intermediate scalars with
m>dH/2
, we have
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu>0
. So, in the squeezed limit, the nonlocal signal piece gives rise to oscillatory functions of
r_{1}r_{2}
in the form of
\mathcal{J}_{\text{NS}}^{p_{1}p_{2}}\propto(r_{1}r_{2})^{2+3d/2+p_{12}}\cos[2\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\log(r_{1}r_{2})+\vartheta_{\text{NS}}]
. Here we see that the frequency of the oscillation is precisely
2\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu
at the 1-loop level, which is twice the frequency for a tree-level mediation by a single scalar line of mass parameter
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu
. This confirms the observation originally made in
[
15
]
. Here we have worked out the size and also the phase
\vartheta_{\text{NS}}
associated with this signal. Also, we have found all the subleading corrections to the squeezed-limit result, in the form of power series in
r_{1}r_{2}
and also in the two
\mathbf{F}
functions. We note that this part has also been found analytically in
[
71
]
. The result in
[
71
]
was expressed as a four-layer summation, while our result here has only one layer of summation. An analytical proof of the equivalence of the two results would be nontrivial. We have checked that the two results agree perfectly numerically. Also, we have checked that the two results agree analytically at several leading terms in the powers of
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
.

Local signal.

The local signal is completely from the spectral integral (
35
) over the local tree seed integral through the Set-2A poles. See (
49
) and (
3.3
). The local signal
\mathcal{J}_{\text{LS}}^{p_{1}p_{2}}(r_{1},r_{2})
contains a factor
(r_{1}/r_{2})^{\pm 2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}
in the squeezed limit, and thus is nonanalytic in either
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
, but is analytic in the intermediate momentum
k_{s}
(since
k_{s}
is canceled in the ratio
r_{1}/r_{2}
). The full result is:

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{\text{LS}}^{p_{1}p_{2}}=

\displaystyle~\frac{(r_{1}/r_{2})^{d/2+2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}\sin[\pi(\mbox{$\frac{\,{d}\,}{2}$}+2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)]}{8\pi^{d/2}\Gamma\big(\tfrac{d}{2}\big)\sin^{2}(\pi\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\displaystyle\frac{\,{(1+n)_{\frac{d}{2}-1}\big[(1+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+n)_{\frac{d}{2}-1}\big]^{2}(1+2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+n)_{\frac{d}{2}-1}}\,}{(1+2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+2n)_{d-1}}

\displaystyle\times(\mbox{$\frac{\,{d}\,}{2}$}+2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+2n)\mathcal{C}_{2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+d/2+2n,d}^{p_{1}p_{2}}\mathbf{F}_{2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+d/2+2n,d}^{p_{1}}(r_{1})\mathbf{F}_{-2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu-d/2-2n,d}^{p_{2}}(r_{2})(r_{1}/r_{2})^{2n}+\text{c.c.}.

(62)

Again, the local signal generates an oscillatory behavior
\propto(r_{1}/r_{2})^{2+3d/2+p_{12}}\cos[2\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\log(r_{1}/r_{2})+\vartheta_{\text{LS}}]
. To our best knowledge, the local CC signals in 1-loop processes have not been worked out in the literature, and we believe that our result for
\mathcal{J}_{\text{LS}}
is new.

Logarithmic tail.

The logarithmic tail is from the spectral integrals over the nonlocal and local tree seed integral, namely (
34
) and (
35
), through the poles in Set 1C and Set 2C. The “nonlocal” tail
\propto\log r_{1}r_{2}
and the “local” tail
\propto\log(r_{1}/r_{2})
can be combined into a single term, which we call the logarithmic tail:

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{\text{LT}}^{p_{1}p_{2}}=

\displaystyle~\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{Z}_{n,d}^{p_{1}p_{2}}\Pi_{n,d}(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)\mathbf{F}_{n,d}^{p_{1}}(r_{1})\mathbf{F}_{n,d}^{p_{2}}(r_{2})(r_{1}r_{2})^{n}\log r_{2}.

(63)

Recall that
r_{2}=k_{s}/k_{34}
. Thus the logarithmic tail is nonanalytic in
k_{s}
as
k_{s}\rightarrow 0
but does not exhibit any oscillatory behavior in momentum ratios. The existence of the logarithmic tail is a special feature of the 1-loop correlator, which has no counterparts in tree-level correlators. However, as we show in App.
B.4
, in the case of
d=3
, the function
\Pi_{n,3}(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)=0
when
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. Therefore, the logarithmic tail vanishes identically in
(3+1)
-dimensional dS, although it does not vanish in general
d
. For example, the logarithmic tail is nonzero in
d=2
.

Background.

Finally, the background piece
\mathcal{J}_{\text{BG}}^{p_{1}p_{2}}
is fully analytic in
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
around
r_{1,2}=0
. It receives contributions from all three integrals in (
34
)-(
36
). We can put it in the following way:

\mathcal{J}_{\text{BG}}^{p_{1}p_{2}}=\mathcal{J}_{\text{(3)}}+\mathcal{J}_{\text{(B)}}+\mathcal{J}_{(\text{C})}+\mathcal{J}_{\text{(2D)}}.

(64)

Here
\mathcal{J}_{\text{(3)}}
is from the integral (
36
), namely, it is from the background tree integral (
31
).

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{\text{(3)}}=

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell,m=0}^{\infty}\sum_{n=0}^{m}\frac{(-1)^{\ell+n}\sin[\frac{\pi}{2}(p_{12}+d)](\ell+1)_{2m+d+p_{1}+p_{2}+1}}{2^{2m}n!(m-n)!\big(p_{2}+1+\mbox{$\frac{\,{d}\,}{2}$}+\ell+n\big)_{m+1}}\big(\mbox{$\frac{\,{1}\,}{2}$}d+p_{2}+1+\ell+2n\big)

\displaystyle\times\rho_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{\text{dS}}(-\mbox{$\frac{\,{\mathrm{i}d}\,}{2}$}-\mathrm{i}p_{2}-\mathrm{i}-\mathrm{i}\ell-2\mathrm{i}n)r_{1}^{2m+d+p_{12}+2}\Big(\frac{r_{1}}{r_{2}}\Big)^{\ell}.

(65)

As we shall see,
\mathcal{J}_{\text{(3)}}
is the only term in the loop seed integral that is divergent when
d\rightarrow 3
.

Next,
\mathcal{J}_{\text{(B)}}
comes from the Set 1B and Set 2B poles of
\mathcal{J}_{\text{(1)}}
and
\mathcal{J}_{\text{(2)}}
, respectively:

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{\text{(B)}}=

\displaystyle~\frac{\sin\mbox{$\frac{\,{\pi d}\,}{2}$}\csc^{2}(\pi\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)}{4\pi^{d/2}\Gamma\big(\tfrac{d}{2}\big)}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\displaystyle\frac{\,{\big[(1+n)_{\frac{d}{2}-1}\big]^{2}(1+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+n)_{\frac{d}{2}-1}(1-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+n)_{\frac{d}{2}-1}}\,}{(1+2n)_{d-1}}(\mbox{$\frac{\,{d}\,}{2}$}+2n)\mathcal{C}_{d/2+2n,d}^{p_{1}p_{2}}

\displaystyle\times\mathbf{F}_{d/2+2n,d}^{p_{1}}(r_{1})r_{1}^{2n+d/2}\Big[\mathbf{F}_{d/2+2n,d}^{p_{2}}(r_{2})r_{2}^{2n+d/2}+\mathbf{F}_{-d/2-2n,d}^{p_{2}}(r_{2})r_{2}^{-2n-d/2}\Big].

(66)

The third piece
\mathcal{J}_{(\text{C})}
comes from the Set 1C and Set 2C poles of
\mathcal{J}_{\text{(1)}}
and
\mathcal{J}_{\text{(2)}}
, respectively. Most terms in (
3.2
) and (
3.3
) combine to zero, and we end up with the following result:

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{(\text{C})}=

\displaystyle~\frac{1}{8\pi^{2}}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{Z}_{n,d}^{p_{1}p_{2}}\Pi_{n,d}(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)\mathbf{F}_{n,d}^{p_{1}}(r_{1})

\displaystyle\times\Big\{\mathbf{G}_{n,d}^{p_{2}}(r_{2})\mathbf{F}_{n,d}^{p_{2}}(r_{2})(r_{1}r_{2})^{n}+\mathbf{G}_{-n,d}^{p_{2}}(r_{2})\mathbf{F}_{-n,d}^{p_{2}}(r_{2})\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{r_{1}}\,}{r_{2}}\Big)^{n}\Big\}.

(67)

When
d\rightarrow 3
, this result also vanishes since
\Pi_{n,d}(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)=0
when
d=3
and
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Finally, the piece
\mathcal{J}_{\text{(2D)}}
comes from the Set 2D poles of the integral
\mathcal{J}_{(2)}
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{\text{(2D)}}=

\displaystyle~(2r_{1})^{d/2+1+p_{2}}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{n}2^{1+n}\sqrt{\pi}\big(\frac{d}{2}+p_{2}+1+n\big)}{n!}\Pi_{d/2+p_{2}+1+n,d}(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)

\displaystyle\times\mathcal{C}_{d/2+p_{2}+1+n,d}^{p_{1}p_{2}}\mathbf{F}_{d/2+p_{2}+1+n,d}^{p_{1}}(r_{1}){\,}_{2}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mathrm{F}\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1}\left[\begin{matrix}-\tfrac{n}{2},\tfrac{1-n}{2}\\
-\tfrac{d}{2}-p_{2}-n\end{matrix}\middle|r_{2}^{2}\right]\Big(\frac{r_{1}}{r_{2}}\Big)^{n}.

(68)

In
d=3
and integer
p_{2}
, this piece involves the
\Pi_{n,d}
function with
n+1/2\in\mathbb{Z}
. As we can see from the explicit expression of
\Pi_{n,3}
in (
162
),
\Pi_{1/2,3}
is finite when
n=1/2
;
\Pi_{n,3}=0
when
n=2k+1/2
with
k=1,2,\cdots
, and is divergent as a simple pole when
n=2k+3/2
with
k=0,1,2,\cdots
. However, these poles happen to be zeros of the second degree of the factor
\mathcal{C}_{d/2+p_{2}+1+n,d}^{p_{1}p_{2}}
when
p_{1,2}
are even integers, which is always true for the special examples considered in this work. Therefore, when
d=3
and
p_{1},p_{2}
being even integers,
\mathcal{J}_{\text{(2D)}}
vanishes unless
p_{2}
taking a value equal to or smaller than
-2
. When
p_{2}=-2
, there will be a single term with
n=0
in (
3.5
) that contributes to the background. This happens in several examples considered in Sec.
2
; See (
18
), (
20
), and (
22
). On the other hand, we shall never encounter
p_{2}<-2
in this work.

At this point, we have finished the computation of the loop seed integral. The result is rather complicated in general
d
dimensions, as is summarized in (
60
). We shall examine its property and make several consistency checks in the next section, at least to make sure that our result reduces to known results in certain limits. Then, in Sec.
5
, we shall use the loop seed integral to compute several realistic 1-loop inflaton correlators in
d=3
. There we shall get significantly simplified expressions.

4  Properties and Consistency Checks

In this section, we discuss the properties of the loop seed integrals in several limits, including the
d\rightarrow 3
limit, the large mass (
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\gg 1
) limit, the squeezed limit where
r_{1},r_{2}\rightarrow 0
, the folded limit where
r_{1}
or
r_{2}\rightarrow 1
, and the
r_{1}=r_{2}
limit. In these limits, either the loop seed integral can be (at least partially) computed using other methods, or it should have expected properties on physical grounds. Therefore, examining these limits can serve as consistency checks of our results.

4.1
Divergence in 3+1 dimensions

While the seed integral is computed in arbitrary
d
spatial dimensions, we are ultimately interested in the case of
d=3
. In dS
3+1
, the UV divergence is expected to appear in the 1-loop correlator. In the loop seed integral
\mathcal{J}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{p_{1}p_{2}}
in (
60
), the nonlocal signal
\mathcal{J}_{\text{NS}}^{p_{1}p_{2}}
and the local signal
\mathcal{J}_{\text{LS}}^{p_{1}p_{2}}
are manifestly finite when
d\rightarrow 3
, as one can directly see from (
3.5
) and (
3.5
). The logarithmic tail
\mathcal{J}_{\text{LT}}^{p_{1}p_{2}}
vanishes when
d\rightarrow 3
, due to the vanishing of the
\Pi
function:
\Pi_{n,d}(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)=0
at
d=3
and integer
n
. So any possible UV divergence must come from the background piece
\mathcal{J}_{\text{BG}}^{p_{1}p_{2}}
, as we should expect.

For the background piece
\mathcal{J}_{\text{BG}}^{p_{1}p_{2}}
in (
64
), we can see that the last three terms, including
\mathcal{J}_{\text{(B)}}
,
\mathcal{J}_{(\text{C})}
, and
\mathcal{J}_{\text{(2D)}}
, all remain finite or vanish at
d\rightarrow 3
. Therefore, the only potential UV divergence comes from the first term
\mathcal{J}_{(3)}
, which is explicitly given in (
3.5
). Closer examination shows that the divergence of
\mathcal{J}_{(3)}
comes from the spectral density
\rho_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{\text{dS}}(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{\prime})
. As we show in App.
B.3
, when
d\rightarrow 3
, the spectral density behaves like:

\lim_{d\rightarrow 3}\rho_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{\text{dS}}(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{\prime})\sim-\displaystyle\frac{\,{1}\,}{(4\pi)^{2}}\displaystyle\frac{\,{2}\,}{3-d}+\text{finite terms}.

(69)

As expected, the divergent part is independent of either
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu
or
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{\prime}
, as UV physics should be insensitive to physics at finite scales. Now, we insert (
69
) back into
\mathcal{J}_{\text{(3)}}
in (
3.5
), and find:

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{\text{(3)}}=

\displaystyle~\displaystyle\frac{\,{1}\,}{(4\pi)^{2}}\displaystyle\frac{\,{2}\,}{3-d}\sum_{\ell,m=0}^{\infty}\sum_{n=0}^{m}\frac{(-1)^{\ell+n}\cos\mbox{$\frac{\,{\pi p_{12}}\,}{2}$}(\ell+1)_{2m+p_{12}+4}}{2^{2m}n!(m-n)!\big(p_{2}+\mbox{$\frac{\,{5}\,}{2}$}+\ell+n\big)_{m+1}}

\displaystyle\times\big(p_{2}+\mbox{$\frac{\,{5}\,}{2}$}+\ell+2n\big)r_{1}^{2m+p_{12}+5}\Big(\frac{r_{1}}{r_{2}}\Big)^{\ell}+\mathcal{O}\Big((3-d)^{0}\Big).

(70)

At this point, the summation in (
4.1
) can be completed, because the
n
-sum is zero except when
m=0
. To see this, consider the following slightly more general summation:

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{M}\frac{(-1)^{n}(p_{2}+\mbox{$\frac{\,{5}\,}{2}$}+\ell+2n)}{n!(m-n)!\big(p_{2}+\mbox{$\frac{\,{5}\,}{2}$}+\ell+n\big)_{m+1}}=\displaystyle\frac{\,{(-1)^{M}}\,}{m}\Gamma\begin{bmatrix}p_{2}+\mbox{$\frac{\,{7}\,}{2}$}+\ell+M\\
1+M,m-M,p_{2}+\mbox{$\frac{\,{7}\,}{2}$}+\ell+M+m\end{bmatrix}.

(71)

The
n
-sum in (
4.1
) is obtained by setting
M=m
, which gives zero when
m\neq 0
, due to an Euler
\Gamma
factor
\Gamma(m-M)
in the denominator. However, when
m=0
, the two factors,
m
and
\Gamma(m-M)
, are combined to give a finite result. Therefore, we see that we only need to retain the
m=0
term in the summation in (
4.1
), and the result is:

\mathcal{J}_{\text{(3)}}=\displaystyle\frac{\,{1}\,}{(4\pi)^{2}}\displaystyle\frac{\,{2}\,}{3-d}\cos\big(\mbox{$\frac{\,{\pi p_{12}}\,}{2}$}\big)\Gamma(5+p_{12})\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{r_{1}r_{2}}\,}{r_{1}+r_{2}}\Big)^{5+p_{12}}+\mathcal{O}\Big((3-d)^{0}\Big).

(72)

Note that the combination
r_{1}r_{2}/(r_{1}+r_{2})=k_{s}/k_{1234}
is nothing but the factor we would expect to see from the 4-point correlator generated by a contact interaction. This shows that the UV divergence of the 1-loop correlator is completely local and can be subtracted by a local counterterm.

To be more specific, we can take an example with four conformal scalars directly coupled to the massive scalars. As shown in (
18
), this corresponds to
p_{1}=p_{2}=-2
. So, the above expression gives

\mathcal{L}_{\phi_{c},\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}\Big|_{s,\text{div.}}=\displaystyle\frac{\,{\tau_{f}^{4}}\,}{16k_{1}k_{2}k_{3}k_{4}k_{s}}\mathcal{J}_{\text{(3)}}^{-2,-2}=\displaystyle\frac{\,{1}\,}{(4\pi)^{2}}\displaystyle\frac{\,{2}\,}{3-d}\displaystyle\frac{\,{\tau_{f}^{4}}\,}{16k_{1}k_{2}k_{3}k_{4}k_{1234}}+\mathcal{O}\Big((3-d)^{0}\Big).

(73)

There are two identical divergent contributions from the
t
-channel and
u
-channel exchanges. So the total result for the UV divergence is the above expression multiplied by three. On the other hand, we can directly compute the 4-point correlator of conformal scalars with direct quartic interaction
\Delta{\mathscr{L}}=-\mbox{$\frac{\,{1}\,}{24}$}\delta_{\lambda}\phi_{c}^{4}
, with
\delta_{\lambda}
understood as the coefficient of the counterterm. It is straightforward to find the result from a bulk calculation:

\displaystyle\langle\phi_{c,\mathbf{k}_{1}}\phi_{c,\mathbf{k}_{2}}\phi_{c,\mathbf{k}_{3}}\phi_{c,\mathbf{k}_{4}}\rangle^{\prime}=

\displaystyle-\mathrm{i}\delta_{\lambda}\sum_{\mathsf{a}=\pm}\mathsf{a}\int_{-\infty}^{\tau_{f}}\displaystyle\frac{\,{{\mathrm{d}}\tau}\,}{\tau^{4}}C_{\mathsf{a}}(k_{1},\tau)C_{\mathsf{a}}(k_{2},\tau)C_{\mathsf{a}}(k_{3},\tau)C_{\mathsf{a}}(k_{4},\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\displaystyle\frac{\,{-\delta_{\lambda}\tau_{f}^{4}}\,}{8k_{1}k_{2}k_{3}k_{4}k_{1234}}.

(74)

Therefore, we see that the UV divergence can be subtracted by a local counterterm
-\mbox{$\frac{\,{1}\,}{24}$}\delta_{\lambda}\phi_{c}^{4}
with
\delta_{\lambda}=3/[(4\pi)^{2}(3-d)]
, just as what we would expect from a flat-space computation. The reason for this agreement is clear: For a massive loop, the divergence at
d=3
is totally from the physics in the ultraviolet region of the momentum space, where the finite curvature of the spacetime should be negligible.

4.2
Large mass limit

As shown in the previous subsection, the flat-space intuition applies to the UV divergent part of the 1-loop correlator. Moreover, when the mass
m
running in the loop is much greater than the Hubble scale
H
, we should also expect that the
finite
part of the dS 1-loop correlator approaches the corresponding result in the flat space. The easiest way to see this is that there are only two scales involved in our problem, namely the mass
m
and the Hubble scale
H
. Therefore, taking the large mass limit
m/H\gg 1
is equivalent to sending
H\rightarrow 0
, which is just the flat-space limit.

Even in the Minkowski space, the 1-loop in-in correlator is not a very familiar result. It turns out that a direct computation via the standard diagrammatic rule in the SK formalism is not trivial in flat space. In App.
F
, we use the spectral decomposition method to compute the same 1-loop correlator in Fig.
1
with the direct coupling
-\frac{1}{4}\varphi^{2}\sigma^{2}
but in Minkowski spacetime. Here we quote the result in the
d\rightarrow 3
limit:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{m}^{\text{Mink}}=

\displaystyle~\displaystyle\frac{\,{1}\,}{256\pi^{2}E_{1}E_{2}E_{3}E_{4}E_{1234}}\bigg[\displaystyle\frac{\,{2}\,}{3-d}-\gamma_{E}+\log 4\pi+2

\displaystyle~+\displaystyle\frac{\,{2}\,}{E_{12}-E_{34}}\int_{0}^{1}{\mathrm{d}}\xi\,\bigg(E_{34}\log\displaystyle\frac{\,{E_{12}+E_{\text{min}}}\,}{\mu_{R}}-E_{12}\log\displaystyle\frac{\,{E_{34}+E_{\text{min}}}\,}{\mu_{R}}\bigg)\bigg].

(75)

Here
E_{i}\equiv\sqrt{\mathbf{k}_{i}^{2}+m_{i}^{2}}
with
i=1,2,3,4
, (It does not bring any pain to add nonzero masses to external fields in this case.) and
E_{\text{min}}\equiv\sqrt{\mathbf{k}_{s}^{2}+m^{2}/[\xi(1-\xi)]}
. The mass scale
\mu_{R}
is a renormalization scale, and can be thought of as coming from the mass dimension of the coupling constant in
d
spatial dimensions. In the large mass limit
m\rightarrow\infty
,

\displaystyle\Big[\mathcal{L}_{m}^{\text{Mink}}\Big]_{\overline{\text{MS}}}=

\displaystyle~\displaystyle\frac{\,{1}\,}{256\pi^{2}E_{1}E_{2}E_{3}E_{4}E_{1234}}\bigg[\log\displaystyle\frac{\,{\mu_{R}^{2}}\,}{m^{2}}-\displaystyle\frac{\,{E_{12}E_{34}+\mathbf{k}_{s}^{2}}\,}{6m^{2}}+\mathcal{O}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{1}\,}{m^{3}}\Big)\bigg].

(76)

Now let us return to the dS case. Let us define a renormalized spectral function under the modified minimal subtraction scheme (
\overline{\text{MS}}
):

\displaystyle\mkern 2.0mu\widehat{\mkern-2.0mu\rho\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{\text{dS}}(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{\prime})\equiv\lim_{d\rightarrow 3}\bigg[\rho_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{\text{dS}}(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{\prime})+\displaystyle\frac{\,{1}\,}{(4\pi)^{2}}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{2}\,}{3-d}-\gamma_{E}+\log 4\pi\Big)\bigg].

(77)

In App.
C
, we show that the large mass limit (
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\gg 1
) of the renormalized spectral function
\mkern 2.0mu\widehat{\mkern-2.0mu\rho\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{\text{dS}}(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{\prime})
with
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{\prime}
held fixed is given by (
186
). Specialized to
d=3
, the result is:

\lim_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\rightarrow\infty}\mkern 2.0mu\widehat{\mkern-2.0mu\rho\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{\text{dS}}(-\mathrm{i}\nu^{\prime})\sim\displaystyle\frac{\,{1}\,}{(4\pi)^{2}}\bigg[\log(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{2})+\mathcal{O}({\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{-2}})\bigg],

(78)

where the logarithmic term is obtained from explicit analytical derivations, while the
1/\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{2}
term can be obtained from a numerical fit. To get a loop correlator that can be directly compared with the flat-space result (
76
), we again take the example of the non-derivatively coupled conformal scalar in (
18
). We replace the spectral function
\rho_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{\text{dS}}
in
\mathcal{J}_{\text{(3)}}
by (
78
), and take
p_{1}=p_{2}=-2
. Then, we get:

\lim_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\rightarrow\infty}\displaystyle\frac{\,{\tau_{f}^{4}}\,}{16k_{1}k_{2}k_{3}k_{4}k_{s}}\mathcal{J}_{\text{(3)}}^{-2,-2}\sim-\displaystyle\frac{\,{1}\,}{16k_{1}k_{2}k_{3}k_{4}k_{1234}}\displaystyle\frac{\,{1}\,}{(4\pi)^{2}}\Big[\log\displaystyle\frac{\,{m^{2}}\,}{\mu_{R}^{2}}+\mathcal{O}({\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{-2}})\Big].

(79)

Here we have set
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\simeq m/H
in the final expression. Also, we have introduced a renormalization scale
\mu_{R}
, which comes from the dimension of the coupling constant in general
d
dimensions. It is clear that the coefficient of
\log(m^{2}/\mu_{R}^{2})
in (
79
) matches exactly the coefficient of
\log(m^{2}/\mu_{R}^{2})
in (
76
), as expected.

4.3
Squeezed limit

Now we consider a particular kinematic configuration, called the squeezed limit, where
r_{1,2}\ll 1
, namely, the momentum
k_{s}
mediated by the loop is much smaller than any of the external momenta
k_{i}
. This is the limit of most interest to CC physics, where we expect to see oscillatory signals. From a bulk perspective, the signal is contributed dominantly by the superhorizon modes of the loop particles, and therefore it is possible to compute at least the nonlocal signal by taking the late-time expansion of the loop propagator. Below we shall perform this late-time computation directly in the bulk, and compare the resulting nonlocal signal with the leading term in
\mathcal{J}_{\text{NS}}^{p_{1}p_{2}}
in (
3.5
).

It turns out that the late-time computation is most easily done in position space. Therefore, we Fourier-transform the loop momentum integral (
9
) back to position space:
9
9

9

After the Fourier transformation, the SK indices
\mathsf{a}\mathsf{b}
of the momentum-space propagator
D_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu,\mathsf{a}\mathsf{b}}
are translated to various types of
\mathrm{i}\epsilon
-prescriptions for the coordinates. The detail is irrelevant here, since the required nonlocal part of the propagator is real and is independent of SK indices. See (
82
).

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu,\mathsf{ab}}\big(k_{s};\tau_{1},\tau_{2}\big)=

\displaystyle\int\displaystyle\frac{\,{{\mathrm{d}}^{d}\mathbf{q}}\,}{(2\pi)^{d}}D_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu,\mathsf{ab}}\Big(q;\tau_{1},\tau_{2}\Big)D_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu,\mathsf{ab}}\Big(|\mathbf{k}_{s}-\mathbf{q}|;\tau_{1},\tau_{2}\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int{\mathrm{d}}^{d}\mathbf{x}\,e^{\mathrm{i}\mathbf{k}_{s}\cdot\mathbf{x}}\Big[D_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}(\tau_{1},\mathbf{x};\tau_{2},\mathbf{0})\Big]^{2},

(80)

where
D_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}(\tau_{1},\mathbf{x}_{1};\tau_{2},\mathbf{x}_{2})
is the position-space massive scalar propagator in
d
spatial dimensions:

\displaystyle D_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}(\tau_{1},\mathbf{x}_{1};\tau_{2},\mathbf{x}_{2})=\displaystyle\frac{\,{1}\,}{(4\pi)^{(d+1)/2}}{}_{2}\mathcal{F}_{1}\left[\begin{matrix}\mbox{$\frac{\,{d}\,}{2}$}+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu,\mbox{$\frac{\,{d}\,}{2}$}-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\\
\mbox{$\frac{\,{d+1}\,}{2}$}\end{matrix}\middle|\displaystyle\frac{\,{(\tau_{1}+\tau_{2})^{2}-|\mathbf{x}_{1}-\mathbf{x}_{2}|^{2}}\,}{4\tau_{1}\tau_{2}}\right].

(81)

The good thing is that the loop momentum integral reduces to the square of a single propagator. Therefore, we can simply make the late-time expansion of the propagator at
\tau_{1,2}\rightarrow 0
, from which we get:

\displaystyle\Big[D_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}(\tau_{1},\mathbf{x};\tau_{2},\mathbf{0})\Big]_{\text{NL}}^{2}\sim\displaystyle\frac{\,{1}\,}{16\pi^{d+2}}\Gamma^{2}\big[\mbox{$\frac{\,{d}\,}{2}$}+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu,-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\big]\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{\tau_{1}\tau_{2}}\,}{\mathbf{x}^{2}}\Big)^{d+2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}+\text{c.c.}.

(82)

Here the notation
[\cdots]_{\text{NL}}
means that we only retain nonlocal terms, i.e., terms of noninteger powers of
\mathbf{x}^{2}
, with the anticipation that such terms are the only source of the nonlocal signal.

Then the nonlocal part of the loop momentum integral can be directly got by substituting (
82
) back into (
4.3
), and the result is:

\displaystyle\big[\mathcal{Q}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}\big]_{\text{NL}}\sim\displaystyle\frac{\,{(k_{s}^{2}\tau_{1}\tau_{2})^{d+2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}}\,}{8\pi^{(d+5)/2}k_{s}^{d}}\cos\big[\mbox{$\frac{\,{\pi}\,}{2}$}(d+4\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)\big]\Gamma^{2}\big[\mbox{$\frac{\,{d}\,}{2}$}+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu,-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\big]\Gamma\begin{bmatrix}\mbox{$\frac{\,{1+d}\,}{2}$}+2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu,-d-4\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\\
d+2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\end{bmatrix}+\text{c.c.}.

(83)

Note in particular that the nonlocal part of the loop momentum integral is independent of the SK indices, a feature we should expect. Then, the bulk time integral in (
17
) can be directly finished, and we get:

\displaystyle\big[\mathcal{J}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{p_{1}p_{2}}\big]_{\text{NL}}\sim\displaystyle\frac{\,{r_{1}^{p_{1}}r_{2}^{p_{2}}(r_{1}r_{2})^{1+d+2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}}\,}{2^{1+d+2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}\pi^{2+d/2}}\cos\big[\mbox{$\frac{\,{\pi}\,}{2}$}(d+p_{1}+2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)\big]\cos\big[\mbox{$\frac{\,{\pi}\,}{2}$}(d+p_{2}+2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)\big]\cos\big[\mbox{$\frac{\,{\pi}\,}{2}$}(d+4\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)\big]

\displaystyle\times\Gamma^{2}(-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)\Gamma\begin{bmatrix}1+d+p_{1}+2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu,1+d+p_{2}+2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu,-d-4\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu,\mbox{$\frac{\,{1+d}\,}{2}$}+2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu,\mbox{$\frac{\,{d}\,}{2}$}+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\\
\mbox{$\frac{\,{1+d}\,}{2}$}+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\end{bmatrix}+\text{c.c.}.

(84)

This agrees exactly with the leading (
n=0
) term of the nonlocal signal in (
3.5
).

We note that the computation of the local signal and the background from the late-time expansion would be nontrivial, in part because these pieces are contributed by the “local” terms of the propagator
D(\tau_{1},\mathbf{x},\tau_{2},\mathbf{0})
, namely, the terms analytic in
\mathbf{x}^{2}
. When Fourier-transforming such terms back to the momentum space, one encounters a divergence, which is essentially the UV divergence of the original loop momentum integral. In comparison, the nonlocal signal is automatically free from such divergences and thus is more tractable in the late-time calculation.

Figure 3:
The convergence of the loop seed integral. In the left panel, we show the convergence of the signals
\mathcal{J}_{\text{NS}}^{-2,-2}+\mathcal{J}_{\text{LS}}^{-2,-2}
(magenta) and the background
\mathcal{J}_{\text{BG}}^{-2,-2}
(blue) separately. In each of the two classes of curves, the three curves from lighter to darker colors show the sums of the series up to
n
’th power of
r_{1}/r_{2}
, with
n=5,15,25
, respectively. In the right panel, we show the sum of the signals and the background, again, with three choices of
n
. The grey dotted curve shows the interpolated function of the whole seed integral. In this plot we fix
d=3
,
p_{1}=p_{2}=-2
,
r_{2}=0.3
, and
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu=2
.

4.4
Folded limit and
\bm{r_{1}=r_{2}}
limit

Now we briefly comment on the two limits where our expressions exhibit superficial divergences. One is the folded limit, the other is the
r_{1}=r_{2}
limit.

The folded limit of the loop seed integral means that one or both of
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
go to 1 from below. (Note that the momentum conservation at both vertices of the 1-loop diagram requires that
0\leq r_{1,2}\leq 1
.) All terms in our final result for the loop seed integral (
60
) are expressed as series or partially resummed power series in
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
, and we would expect many of these series to be divergent as
r_{1,2}\rightarrow 1
. On the other hand, we expect that the 1-loop correlator is free from any divergences in the folded limit, as a consequence of choosing Bunch-Davies initial state for all the modes under consideration. Therefore, it would be useful to show that all superficial divergences in the folded limit cancel in (
60
). Our procedure of spectral decomposition suggests that we can check the cancelation of folded divergences directly at the tree level, which was done in previous works
[
58
,
72
]
. Then, so long as taking the folded limit commutes with the spectral integral which we shall assume, it would follow automatically that the loop correlator is also free from folded divergence. We also note that sending the single-side folded limit (
r_{2}\rightarrow 1^{-}
while keeping
r_{1}
fixed) is simple enough. We shall do this explicitly in the next section when computing a 3-point inflaton correlator.

Finally, let us look at the
r_{1}=r_{2}
limit. Physically, we expect our result of the loop seed integral to be smooth at this point. Also, the tree seed integral is smooth at
r_{1}=r_{2}
, although some pieces (the local signal and the background) in the tree seed integral may exhibit non-smooth behavior at
r_{1}=r_{2}
. This non-smooth behavior is purely an artifact of our definition of the local signal and the background. See the comments below (
28
). On the other hand, the various terms of the loop seed integral in (
60
) are expressed in power series of
r_{1}/r_{2}
whose convergences at
r_{1}/r_{2}=1
are far from clear. This superficial discontinuity may be resolved by resumming the series, as we can do for the tree correlators with the Partial Mellin-Barnes representation. (See App.
D
.) We leave this for a future work. For the moment, we only note that this superficial discontinuity does not bring any obstacle to numerical implementation. This is shown in Fig.
3
, where we show the convergence of the various series in (
60
), and in particular, the discontinuity as
r_{1}
goes across
r_{2}
. From this figure, we see that the signal series in (
3.5
) and (
3.5
) converge very quickly. On the other hand, the background series
\mathcal{J}_{\text{BG}}^{-2,-2}
in (
3.5
) converges rather slow around
r_{1}=r_{2}
. However, it is easy to get an interpolated function that smoothly joins the two sides of
r_{1}=r_{2}
where the series converges quickly. This shows that our result can be easily implemented in numerical computations.

5  Applications to Cosmological Collider Physics

With the full result for the loop seed integral at hand, we can directly compute many 1-loop inflaton correlators mediated by massive scalar fields with various types of couplings. In this section, we provide full results for two examples that are most relevant to the study of CC physics. One is the 1-loop trispectrum
\mathcal{L}_{\varphi,\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}
in (
19
), and the other is the 1-loop bispectrum
\mathcal{B}_{\varphi,\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}
in (
20
).

5.1
1-loop trispectrum

Here we present the result for the 1-loop inflaton correlator in Fig.
1
, with the coupling given in (
6
). As suggested by the expression (
19
), the result can be found from (
60
) by setting
p_{1}=p_{2}=0
, and then taking the
d\rightarrow 3
limit. Many terms in (
60
) drop out in this limit, and the result is much simplified compared to the full seed integral. In particular, the logarithmic tail vanishes identically for this process.

It turns out that the result can be more conveniently written in the following way:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\varphi,\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}=\displaystyle\frac{\,{1}\,}{16k_{1}k_{2}k_{3}k_{4}(k_{12}k_{34})^{5/2}}\Big[\mkern 2.0mu\widehat{\mkern-2.0mu\mathcal{J}\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\!_{\text{NS}}(r_{1},r_{2})+\mkern 2.0mu\widehat{\mkern-2.0mu\mathcal{J}\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\!_{\text{LS}}(r_{1},r_{2})+\mkern 2.0mu\widehat{\mkern-2.0mu\mathcal{J}\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\!_{\text{BG}}(r_{1},r_{2})\Big].

(85)

Here
\mkern 2.0mu\widehat{\mkern-2.0mu\mathcal{J}\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\!_{\text{NS}}\equiv(r_{1}r_{2})^{-5/2}\mathcal{J}_{\text{NS}}^{00}
with the unhatted
\mathcal{J}_{\text{NS}}^{p_{1}p_{2}}
given by (
3.5
). This part represents the nonlocal signal:

\displaystyle\mkern 2.0mu\widehat{\mkern-2.0mu\mathcal{J}\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\!_{\text{NS}}=

\displaystyle~\frac{2(r_{1}r_{2})^{3/2+2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}}{\pi^{2}\cos(2\pi\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\displaystyle\frac{\,{(1+n)_{\frac{1}{2}}\big[(1+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+n)_{\frac{1}{2}}\big]^{2}(1+2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+n)_{\frac{1}{2}}}\,}{(1+2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+2n)_{2}}(\mbox{$\frac{\,{3}\,}{2}$}+2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+2n)

\displaystyle~\times{}_{2}\mathcal{F}_{1}\left[\begin{matrix}2+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+n,\mbox{$\frac{\,{5}\,}{2}$}+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+n\\
\mbox{$\frac{\,{5}\,}{2}$}+2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+2n\end{matrix}\middle|r_{1}^{2}\right]{}_{2}\mathcal{F}_{1}\left[\begin{matrix}2+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+n,\mbox{$\frac{\,{5}\,}{2}$}+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+n\\
\mbox{$\frac{\,{5}\,}{2}$}+2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+2n\end{matrix}\middle|r_{2}^{2}\right](r_{1}r_{2})^{2n}+\text{c.c.}.

(86)

Similarly,
\mkern 2.0mu\widehat{\mkern-2.0mu\mathcal{J}\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\!_{\text{LS}}\equiv(r_{1}r_{2})^{-5/2}\mathcal{J}_{\text{LS}}^{00}
with the unhatted
\mathcal{J}_{\text{LS}}^{p_{1}p_{2}}
given by (
3.5
). This part represents the local signal:

\displaystyle\mkern 2.0mu\widehat{\mkern-2.0mu\mathcal{J}\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\!_{\text{LS}}=

\displaystyle-\frac{2(r_{1}/r_{2})^{3/2+2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}}{\pi^{2}\cos(2\pi\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\displaystyle\frac{\,{(1+n)_{\frac{1}{2}}\big[(1+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+n)_{\frac{1}{2}}\big]^{2}(1+2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+n)_{\frac{1}{2}}}\,}{(1+2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+2n)_{2}}(\mbox{$\frac{\,{3}\,}{2}$}+2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+2n)

\displaystyle~\times{}_{2}\mathcal{F}_{1}\left[\begin{matrix}2+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+n,\mbox{$\frac{\,{5}\,}{2}$}+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+n\\
\mbox{$\frac{\,{5}\,}{2}$}+2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+2n\end{matrix}\middle|r_{1}^{2}\right]{}_{2}\mathcal{F}_{1}\left[\begin{matrix}\mbox{$\frac{\,{1}\,}{2}$}-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu-n,1-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu-n\\
-\mbox{$\frac{\,{1}\,}{2}$}-2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu-2n\end{matrix}\middle|r_{2}^{2}\right]\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{r_{1}}\,}{r_{2}}\Big)^{2n}+\text{c.c.}.

(87)

Finally,
\mkern 2.0mu\widehat{\mkern-2.0mu\mathcal{J}\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\!_{\text{BG}}\equiv(r_{1}r_{2})^{-5/2}\mathcal{J}_{\text{BG}}^{00}
with the unhatted
\mathcal{J}_{\text{BG}}^{p_{1}p_{2}}
given by (
64
). It turns out that all terms in (
64
), except
\mathcal{J}_{(3)}
, vanish in
d\rightarrow 3
limit with
p_{1}=p_{2}=0
. On the contrary, the term in
\mathcal{J}_{(3)}
possesses the usual UV divergence as
d\rightarrow 3
. Therefore we use the
\overline{\text{MS}}
spectral function
\mkern 2.0mu\widehat{\mkern-2.0mu\rho\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{\text{dS}}(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{\prime})
in (
77
) in place of the original spectral function, and get the following expression for the background
\mkern 2.0mu\widehat{\mkern-2.0mu\mathcal{J}\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\!_{\text{BG}}
under the
\overline{\text{MS}}
scheme:

\displaystyle\mkern 2.0mu\widehat{\mkern-2.0mu\mathcal{J}\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\!_{\text{BG}}=

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell,m=0}^{\infty}\sum_{n=0}^{m}\frac{(-1)^{\ell+n+1}(\ell+1)_{2m+4}(\mbox{$\frac{\,{5}\,}{2}$}+\ell+2n)}{2^{2m}n!(m-n)!\big(\mbox{$\frac{\,{5}\,}{2}$}+\ell+n\big)_{m+1}}

\displaystyle\times\bigg[\mkern 2.0mu\widehat{\mkern-2.0mu\rho\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{\text{dS}}(-\mbox{$\frac{\,{\mathrm{i}5}\,}{2}$}-\mathrm{i}\ell-2\mathrm{i}n)-\displaystyle\frac{\,{1}\,}{(4\pi)^{2}}\log\mu_{R}^{2}\bigg]r_{1}^{2m}\Big(\frac{r_{1}}{r_{2}}\Big)^{5/2+\ell}.

(88)

An explicit expression for the
\overline{\text{MS}}
spectral function
\mkern 2.0mu\widehat{\mkern-2.0mu\rho\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{\text{dS}}(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{\prime})
is given in (
B.3
), which we find useful for numerical implementation. Here we also restore the renormalization scale
\mu_{R}
, which comes from the change of the mass dimension of the coupling constant when the spatial dimension deviates from
d=3
.
10
10

10

More explicitly, in
d
dimensions, the mass dimensions of scalar fields are given by
[\varphi]=[\sigma]=(d-1)/2
, and the Lagrangian has mass dimension
[{\mathscr{L}}]=d+1
. Thus, the coupling term in the Lagrangian should be written as
\mu_{R}^{3-d}\varphi^{\prime 2}\sigma^{2}/(4\Lambda^{2})
where
\Lambda
is a dim-1 cutoff scale, while the counterterm should be written as
\mu_{R}^{3-d}\varphi^{\prime 4}/(24\Lambda_{c}^{4})
where
\Lambda_{c}^{4}
is another dim-1 cutoff scale. When
d\rightarrow 3
, the two couplings in the 1-loop diagram produce a finite piece
-[2/(4\pi)^{2}]\log\mu_{R}^{2}
when combined with the divergent term
-2/[(4\pi)^{2}(3-d)]
of the spectral function. See (
69
). The counterterm gives another finite piece
+[1/(4\pi)^{2}]\log\mu_{R}^{2}
. Combining the two pieces, we get the total dependence on
\mu_{R}
as
-[1/(4\pi)^{2}]\log\mu_{R}^{2}
.

Squeezed limit.

The squeezed limit is of most interest for CC applications, and therefore it would be useful to have an explicit result for the trispectrum in the squeezed limit. Here we take a “hierarchical” squeezed limit
r_{1}\ll r_{2}\ll 1
in (
85
). Then we can keep the leading terms in both
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
, and also in
r_{1}/r_{2}
for all three terms in (
85
). The result is:

\displaystyle\lim_{r_{1}\ll r_{2}\ll 1}\mkern 2.0mu\widehat{\mkern-2.0mu\mathcal{J}\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\!_{\text{NS}}=

\displaystyle~\displaystyle\frac{\,{4^{1+2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}\sec(2\pi\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)}\,}{\pi^{2}}\displaystyle\frac{\,{(1+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)\Gamma^{2}[\mbox{$\frac{\,{3}\,}{2}$}+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu,\mbox{$\frac{\,{5}\,}{2}$}+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu]}\,}{\Gamma(4+4\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)}(r_{1}r_{2})^{3/2+2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}+\text{c.c.},

(89)

\displaystyle\lim_{r_{1}\ll r_{2}\ll 1}\mkern 2.0mu\widehat{\mkern-2.0mu\mathcal{J}\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\!_{\text{LS}}=

\displaystyle-\displaystyle\frac{\,{\sec(2\pi\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)}\,}{4\sqrt{\pi}}\Gamma\begin{bmatrix}\mbox{$\frac{\,{3}\,}{2}$}+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu,\mbox{$\frac{\,{5}\,}{2}$}+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu,1-2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\\
1+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu,1+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu,-\mbox{$\frac{\,{1}\,}{2}$}-2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\end{bmatrix}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{r_{1}}\,}{r_{2}}\Big)^{3/2+2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}+\text{c.c.},

(90)

\displaystyle\lim_{r_{1}\ll r_{2}\ll 1}\mkern 2.0mu\widehat{\mkern-2.0mu\mathcal{J}\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\!_{\text{BG}}=

\displaystyle-24\bigg[\mkern 2.0mu\widehat{\mkern-2.0mu\rho\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{\text{dS}}(-\mbox{$\frac{\,{5\mathrm{i}}\,}{2}$})-\displaystyle\frac{\,{1}\,}{(4\pi)^{2}}\log\mu_{R}^{2}\bigg]\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{r_{1}}\,}{r_{2}}\Big)^{5/2}.

(91)

An interesting observation here is that, if we take the single squeezed limit, i.e., we keep
r_{2}
fixed, and take the
r_{1}\ll r_{2}
limit, then the overall sizes of both the nonlocal and local signals decay as
r_{1}^{3/2}
, while the background decays as
r_{1}^{5/2}
. Therefore, the signals dominate over the background in the single squeezed limit even at the 1-loop level. Although we made this observation from the squeezed-limit results (
89
)-(
91
), this conclusion holds for any fixed
r_{2}
which is not necessarily small.

Figure 4:
The 1-loop inflaton trispectra of 1-loop scalar exchange with mass parameter
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu
. The magenta, blue, and black curves show the signal
\mkern 2.0mu\widehat{\mkern-2.0mu\mathcal{J}\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\!_{\text{NS}}+\mkern 2.0mu\widehat{\mkern-2.0mu\mathcal{J}\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\!_{\text{LS}}
, the background
\mkern 2.0mu\widehat{\mkern-2.0mu\mathcal{J}\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\!_{\text{BG}}
, and the total result
\mkern 2.0mu\widehat{\mkern-2.0mu\mathcal{J}\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\!_{\text{NS}}+\mkern 2.0mu\widehat{\mkern-2.0mu\mathcal{J}\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\!_{\text{LS}}+\mkern 2.0mu\widehat{\mkern-2.0mu\mathcal{J}\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\!_{\text{BG}}
, respectively. In this figure we fix
r_{2}=0.9
and vary
r_{1}\in(10^{-4},10^{-1/2})
. We use the dimensional regularization and the
\overline{\text{MS}}
scheme, with the renormalization scale chosen at
\mu_{R}=\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0muH
.

Squeezed and large mass limit.

It is often useful to further take the large mass limit
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\gg 1
on top of the squeezed limit. In this case, we get

\displaystyle\lim_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\gg 1}\,\lim_{r_{1}\ll r_{2}\ll 1}\mkern 2.0mu\widehat{\mkern-2.0mu\mathcal{J}\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\!_{\text{NS}}=

\displaystyle~\sqrt{\displaystyle\frac{\,{2}\,}{\pi}}e^{-\mathrm{i}\pi/4}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{7/2}e^{-2\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{r_{1}r_{2}}\,}{4}\Big)^{3/2+2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}+\text{c.c.},

(92)

\displaystyle\lim_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\gg 1}\,\lim_{r_{1}\ll r_{2}\ll 1}\mkern 2.0mu\widehat{\mkern-2.0mu\mathcal{J}\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\!_{\text{LS}}=

\displaystyle-\sqrt{\displaystyle\frac{\,{2}\,}{\pi}}e^{+\mathrm{i}\pi/4}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{7/2}e^{-2\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{r_{1}}\,}{r_{2}}\Big)^{3/2+2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}+\text{c.c.},

(93)

\displaystyle\lim_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\gg 1}\,\lim_{r_{1}\ll r_{2}\ll 1}\mkern 2.0mu\widehat{\mkern-2.0mu\mathcal{J}\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\!_{\text{BG}}=

\displaystyle-\displaystyle\frac{\,{3}\,}{2\pi^{2}}\bigg(\log\displaystyle\frac{\,{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{2}}\,}{\mu_{R}^{2}}+\displaystyle\frac{\,{1}\,}{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{2}}\bigg)\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{r_{1}}\,}{r_{2}}\Big)^{5/2}.

(94)

This result is simple enough and can be used for quick estimates of the signal size and observability in phenomenological CC studies.

With the above analytical expressions, we can nicely reproduce several previously known properties of 1-loop correlators: The signals are suppressed by a factor of
e^{-2\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}
due to the doubled Boltzmann suppression of the “on-shell” loop mode in the large mass limit. The background piece, on the other hand, is not exponentially suppressed. Apart from a renormalization-dependent logarithmic term, the background piece scales as
1/\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{2}
. At the same time, our result provides new information that cannot be revealed by a bulk computation with late-time expansion. For example, besides the exponential factor
e^{-2\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}
, we have also fixed the power dependence
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{7/2}
in the signals for both the nonlocal and the local signal. In particular, our result explicitly shows that the local signal, although being analytic in
k_{s}
, is still free from any UV divergence, a fact we should expect but very hard to see directly from the late-time expansion. Also, if we take a late-time expansion of the loop propagator, one would naively suspect that there would be “singly oscillated” signals proportional to
(r_{1}r_{2})^{\pm\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}
or
(r_{1}/r_{2})^{\pm\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}
. Our results show that no such oscillatory terms appear in the final result. Therefore, if such terms are generated in any intermediate step of the late-time calculation, they must be canceled out in the final answer.

In Fig.
4
, we plot the 1-loop trispectrum in (
85
) with the full expressions (
5.1
)-(
5.1
) for several choices of the mass parameter
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu
. In this figure, we fix
r_{2}=0.9
and vary
r_{1}
on a logarithmic scale from
10^{-4}
to
10^{-1/2}
. Here the signal represents the sum of the nonlocal signal and the local signal
\mkern 2.0mu\widehat{\mkern-2.0mu\mathcal{J}\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\!_{\text{NS}}+\mkern 2.0mu\widehat{\mkern-2.0mu\mathcal{J}\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\!_{\text{LS}}
while the background is given by
\mkern 2.0mu\widehat{\mkern-2.0mu\mathcal{J}\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\!_{\text{BG}}
. The dominance of the signal in the squeezed limit (the right side of each panel) is evident in all cases.

5.2
1-loop bispectrum

As the second example of this section, we now look at the 3-point correlator in Fig.
2
. With the interactions given in (
15
), the corresponding bispectrum
\mathcal{B}_{\varphi,\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}
can be written as (
2
). Then, with the explicit result for the loop seed integral (
60
), we can compute the bispectrum
\mathcal{B}_{\varphi,\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}
according to (
20
).

The evaluation of (
20
) involves the folded limit
r_{2}\rightarrow 1^{-}
in the second argument of the loop seed integral. Similar to what we would find from a tree-level process, the folded limit of the local and nonlocal signals diverges, but the divergent pieces cancel themselves. On the other hand, the background part remains finite in the
r_{2}\rightarrow 1
limit. The cancelation of divergence can be checked term by term in the series expressions for
\mathcal{J}_{\text{NS}}
and
\mathcal{J}_{\text{LS}}
. Alternatively, one can also cancel the divergence directly at the tree level, and then perform the spectral integral over the tree-level 3-point function.
11
11

11

See Eq. (256) in
[
72
]
for an explicit result of tree-level 3-point function mediated by a massive scalar field.
Either way, we find the result to be:

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{\varphi,\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}=\displaystyle\frac{\,{1}\,}{8k_{1}k_{2}k_{3}^{4}}\Big[\mathcal{S}_{\text{S}}(r)+\mathcal{S}_{\text{BG}}(r)\Big].

(95)

Here and below, the momentum ratio
r\equiv k_{3}/k_{12}
. The signal
\mathcal{S}_{\text{S}}(r)
is given by the sum
\mathcal{S}_{\text{S}}(r)=\mathcal{J}_{\text{NS}}^{0,-2}(r,1^{-})+\mathcal{J}_{\text{LS}}^{0,-2}(r,1^{-})
, and the explicit result is:

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\text{S}}(r)=

\displaystyle~\displaystyle\frac{\,{r^{4+2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}}\,}{8\pi\sin(-2\pi\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(3+4\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+4n)\displaystyle\frac{\,{(1+n)_{\frac{1}{2}}(1+2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+n)_{\frac{1}{2}}}\,}{(\mbox{$\frac{\,{1}\,}{2}$}+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+n)_{\frac{1}{2}}(\mbox{$\frac{\,{3}\,}{2}$}+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+n)_{\frac{1}{2}}}

\displaystyle~\times{}_{2}\mathcal{F}_{1}\left[\begin{matrix}2+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+n,\tfrac{5}{2}+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+n\\
\tfrac{5}{2}+2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+2n\end{matrix}\middle|r^{2}\right]r^{2n}+\text{c.c.}

(96)

The background part is given by
\mathcal{S}_{\text{BG}}(r)=\mathcal{J}_{\text{BG}}^{0,-2}(r,1^{-})
. This can be obtained from (
64
) by setting
p_{1}=0
,
p_{2}=-2
,
r_{2}=1
and taking the limit
d\rightarrow 3
. Once again, most terms vanish in this limit, except
\mathcal{J}_{\text{(3)}}
in (
3.5
) and the
n=0
term of
\mathcal{J}_{\text{(2D)}}
in (
3.5
). For the UV divergent term, we again use the renormalized spectral function
\mkern 2.0mu\widehat{\mkern-2.0mu\rho\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{\text{dS}}(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{\prime})
in the
\overline{\text{MS}}
scheme. Also, when taking
d\rightarrow 3
limit, it is essential to know the
d=3
result for the
\Pi_{n,d}
function for integer and half-integer
n
, which we work out in App.
B.4
. After a bit of calculation, we find the result of the background to be:

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\text{BG}}(r)=

\displaystyle-\frac{r^{3}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\;\text{csch}(2\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)}{2\pi(1-r^{2})^{2}}+\sum_{\ell,m=0}^{\infty}\sum_{n=0}^{m}\frac{\left(-1\right)^{\ell+n}(\ell+1)_{2m+2}(\mbox{$\frac{\,{1}\,}{2}$}+\ell+2n)}{2^{2m}n!(m-n)!(\mbox{$\frac{\,{1}\,}{2}$}+\ell+n)_{m+1}}

\displaystyle\times\bigg[\mkern 2.0mu\widehat{\mkern-2.0mu\rho\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{\text{dS}}\big(-\mbox{$\frac{\,{\mathrm{i}}\,}{2}$}-\mathrm{i}\ell-2\mathrm{i}n\big)-\displaystyle\frac{\,{1}\,}{(4\pi)^{2}}\log\mu_{R}^{2}\bigg]r^{3+2m+\ell}.

(97)

Here, we also restore the renormalization scale
\mu_{R}
as in the previous example. As expected, the nonlocal and local signal of the 4-point function become degenerate in the 3-point limit, and both contribute the signal piece
\mathcal{S}_{\text{S}}
. Therefore, we see that it is not quite right to use the late-time expansion method to compute the signal in the 3-point correlator, since this late-time calculation can only capture the signal in the
r_{1,2}\rightarrow 0
limit. However, the actual signal in the 3-point function comes from both the nonlocal and local signals with
r_{1}\rightarrow 0
but
r_{2}\rightarrow 1
.

It is again useful to look at the squeezed limit
r\rightarrow 0
, where the bispectrum simplifies to:

\displaystyle\lim_{r\ll 1}\mathcal{S}_{\text{S}}(r)=

\displaystyle~2\csc(-2\pi\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)(1+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)_{\frac{1}{2}}(1+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)_{\frac{3}{2}}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{r}\,}{2}\Big)^{4+2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}+\text{c.c.},

\displaystyle\lim_{r\ll 1}\mathcal{S}_{\text{BG}}(r)=

\displaystyle~2\bigg[\mkern 2.0mu\widehat{\mkern-2.0mu\rho\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{\text{dS}}(-\mbox{$\frac{\,{\mathrm{i}}\,}{2}$})-\displaystyle\frac{\,{1}\,}{(4\pi)^{2}}\log\mu_{R}^{2}-\displaystyle\frac{\,{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\text{csch}(2\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)}\,}{2\pi}\bigg]r^{3}.

(98)

Here we see that, in the squeezed limit
r\rightarrow 0
, the loop signal decays as
r^{4}
while the background decays as
r^{3}
. Therefore, the signal decays faster than the background. This shows that, if we want to look for signals from 1-loop 3-point correlators, we should look at the moderately large momentum ratio
k_{12}/k_{3}
instead of the deep squeezed limit. In other words, there is no parameter space where the signals are dominant. Comparing this behavior with the single squeezed limit of the trispectrum as discussed above, we see that it may be more advantageous to look at the 4-point function if we want to discover CC signals from 1-loop processes.

Finally, we take the large mass limit on top of the squeezed limit. The result is:

\displaystyle\lim_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\gg 1}\,\lim_{r\ll 1}\mathcal{S}_{\text{S}}(r)=

\displaystyle-4\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{2}e^{-2\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{r}\,}{2}\Big)^{4+2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}+\text{c.c.},

(99)

\displaystyle\lim_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\gg 1}\,\lim_{r\ll 1}\mathcal{S}_{\text{BG}}(r)=

\displaystyle~\displaystyle\frac{\,{1}\,}{8\pi^{2}}\bigg(\log\displaystyle\frac{\,{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{2}}\,}{\mu_{R}^{2}}+\displaystyle\frac{\,{1}\,}{25\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{2}}\bigg)r^{3}.

(100)

Again, the signal is suppressed by
e^{-2\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}
and the background is suppressed by
1/\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{2}
, as expected. Furthermore, we can pin down the power dependence
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{2}
in the signal besides the exponential factor.

Figure 5:
The 1-loop inflaton bispectra of 1-loop scalar exchange with mass parameter
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu
. The magenta and black curves show the signal and the total result, respectively. We multiply the signal by a factor of
5
for
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu=1
and a factor of
100
for
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu=2
to make it visible. We use the dimensional regularization and the
\overline{\text{MS}}
scheme, with the renormalization scale chosen at
\mu_{R}=\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0muH
.

In Fig.
5
, we show two examples of the bispectrum from 1-loop scalar exchange with the mass parameter
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu=1
and 2, respectively. Contrary to the case of the trispectrum, the signals in the bispectrum are always too small to be directly visible. This is consistent with what was found from a full numerical approach in
[
73
]
. In Fig.
5
, we multiply the signals by a factor indicated in each panel to make them visible. In practice, when there is a small signal buried in the background, we can use appropriate filtering techniques to dig it out, as demonstrated in
[
73
]
.

6  Conclusions and Outlooks

The inflation correlators at the 1-loop order belong to a largely unexplored area. On the other hand, complete analytical results for these 1-loop correlators can help us to better understand the analytical structures of loop correlators in dS. At the same time, they are also useful for the study of cosmological collider physics. In this work, we have obtained the analytical results for a class of inflation correlators mediated by massive scalar fields at 1-loop order. We use the spectral decomposition to bootstrap the loop correlators directly from tree-level correlators, and thus bypass the difficulty of computing the loop momentum integral. By defining and computing a loop seed integral, we can obtain complete analytical expressions for many correlators with 1-loop massive exchange. We have also identified the single squeezed limit of the 4-point function as a preferred configuration for detecting CC signals from 1-loop processes, where the signals dominate over the background.

There are several directions to be pursued along the line of this work, listed below. We shall address some of them in the next publication, and leave more open questions for future works.

•

In this work, we only considered intermediate states of principal series (
m>dH/2
), which is most relevant to CC physics. It is in principle straightforward to apply our method to fields of complementary series, namely
0<m<dH/2
for scalar fields. We do not expect to see oscillatory signals in this case, and the analytic structure of the spectral integrand is also very different from the principal fields. However, we do expect to see some enhancements of the result for small mass. Therefore they could be of interest to phenomenological studies of inflation physics. See
[
52
]
for an example.

•

We have assumed in this work that the masses of the two loop propagators are equal. Although this is the most encountered case, the two masses in the loop can certainly be different, and it will be interesting to generalize our method to that case as well. An interesting new feature of the 1-loop with masses
\nu_{1}\neq\nu_{2}
is that we can have two classes of oscillatory signals, one with frequency
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1}+\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{2}
, and the other with frequency
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1}-\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{2}
. The phenomenology of unequal-mass loop processes has also been explored previously; See, e.g.,
[
54
]
.

•

It should be straightforward to generalize our method to 1-loop diagrams with nonzero spin exchanges. These are the most relevant 1-loop processes in CC physics. It is also interesting to include boost-breaking effects, such as the helicity-dependent chemical potential, the non-unit sound speed, and the slow-roll corrections. The spectral decomposition technique used in this work relies heavily on the full dS isometry. Thus it would be very interesting to see whether this method can be generalized to boost breaking scenarios.

•

The spectral decomposition used in this work can in principle be applied recursively. With the 1-loop bubble correlator obtained in this work, it is in principle possible to bootstrap multi-loop diagrams, such as the sunset diagram at the 2-loop level.

•

The results presented in this work are expressed as series, or partially resummed series, in several momentum ratios
r_{1}=k_{s}/k_{12}
,
r_{2}=k_{s}/k_{12}
, and
r_{1}/r_{2}=k_{34}/k_{12}
. Although the convergence of the series is guaranteed by construction when these ratios approach 1, many individual terms in the result are superficially divergent, which is inconvenient for numerical implementation. Therefore it would be helpful if we can resum at least part of these power series, as was done in
[
72
]
using the partial Mellin-Barnes representation. We leave these questions for future work.

Appendix

Appendix A
Useful Formulae

In this appendix, we collect definitions and formulae frequently used in the main text and the rest of the appendix. First, we use the following shorthand notations for the products of Euler
\Gamma
functions:

\displaystyle\Gamma\left[z_{1},\cdots,z_{m}\right]\equiv

\displaystyle~\Gamma(z_{1})\cdots\Gamma(z_{m}),

(101)

\displaystyle\Gamma\left[\begin{matrix}z_{1},\cdots,z_{m}\\
w_{1},\cdots,w_{n}\end{matrix}\right]\equiv

\displaystyle~\displaystyle\frac{\,{\Gamma(z_{1})\cdots\Gamma(z_{m})}\,}{\Gamma(w_{1})\cdots\Gamma(w_{n})}.

(102)

The expression
\Gamma[\cdots,\{z\}^{(n)},\cdots]
means that the entry
z
is repeated
n
times in the
\Gamma
products, namely, it represents a factor of
\Gamma^{n}(z)
.

The Pochhammer symbol
(z)_{n}
is frequently used, which is defined by

\displaystyle(z)_{n}\equiv\Gamma\left[\begin{matrix}z+n\\
z\end{matrix}\right].

(103)

In this work, we use various types of (generalized) hypergeometric functions. The original generalized hypergeometric function of
(p,q)
-type is defined by

\displaystyle{}_{p}\mathrm{F}_{q}\left[\begin{matrix}a_{1},\cdots,a_{p}\\
b_{1},\cdots,b_{q}\end{matrix}\middle|z\right]=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\displaystyle\frac{\,{(a_{1})_{n}\cdots(a_{p})_{n}}\,}{(b_{1})_{n}\cdots(b_{q})_{n}}\displaystyle\frac{\,{z^{n}}\,}{n!}.

(104)

We shall also use the regularized hypergeometric function
{}_{p}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mathrm{F}\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{q}
:

{}_{p}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mathrm{F}\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{q}\left[\begin{matrix}a_{1},\cdots,a_{p}\\
b_{1},\cdots,b_{q}\end{matrix}\middle|z\right]=\frac{1}{\Gamma\left[b_{1},\cdots,b_{q}\right]}{}_{p}\mathrm{F}_{q}\left[\begin{matrix}a_{1},\cdots,a_{p}\\
b_{1},\cdots,b_{q}\end{matrix}\middle|z\right].

(105)

The regularized hypergeometric function has the nice property that it is an entire function of all its parameters
(a_{1},\cdots,a_{p},b_{1},\cdots,b_{q})
when
z
is away from the singular points such as
z=1
and
z=\infty
.

We also frequently use the “dressed” hypergeometric function to simplify expressions, which is defined below.

{}_{p}\mathcal{F}_{q}\left[\begin{matrix}a_{1},\cdots,a_{p}\\
b_{1},\cdots,b_{q}\end{matrix}\middle|z\right]=\Gamma\left[\begin{matrix}a_{1},\cdots,a_{p}\\
b_{1},\cdots,b_{q}\end{matrix}\right]{}_{p}\mathrm{F}_{q}\left[\begin{matrix}a_{1},\cdots,a_{p}\\
b_{1},\cdots,b_{q}\end{matrix}\middle|z\right].

(106)

We occasionally need to take derivatives of the hypergeometric functions with respect to their parameters. The first derivative of the hypergeometric function
{}_{p}\mathrm{F}_{q}(a_{1},\cdots,a_{p};b_{1},\cdots,b_{q};z)
with respect to
a_{i}
and
b_{i}
has been worked out in the literature. See, for instance,
[
92
]
:

\displaystyle\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{a_{1}}}{}_{p}\mathrm{F}_{q}\left[\begin{matrix}a_{1},\cdots,a_{p}\\
b_{1},\cdots,b_{q}\end{matrix}\middle|z\right]=

\displaystyle~\displaystyle\frac{\,{z}\,}{a_{1}}\displaystyle\frac{\,{a_{1}\cdots a_{p}}\,}{b_{1}\cdots b_{q}}{}_{p}\Theta_{q}^{(1)}\left[\begin{matrix}1,1\\
a_{1}+1\end{matrix}\middle|\begin{matrix}a_{1},a_{1}+1,\cdots,a_{p}+1\\
2,b_{1}+1,\cdots,b_{q}+1\end{matrix}\middle|z,z\right],

(107)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{b_{1}}}{}_{p}\mathrm{F}_{q}\left[\begin{matrix}a_{1},\cdots,a_{p}\\
b_{1},\cdots,b_{q}\end{matrix}\middle|z\right]=

\displaystyle-\displaystyle\frac{\,{z}\,}{b_{1}}\displaystyle\frac{\,{a_{1}\cdots a_{p}}\,}{b_{1}\cdots b_{q}}{}_{p}\Theta_{q}^{(1)}\left[\begin{matrix}1,1\\
b_{1}+1\end{matrix}\middle|\begin{matrix}b_{1},a_{1}+1,\cdots,a_{p}+1\\
2,b_{1}+1,\cdots,b_{q}+1\end{matrix}\middle|z,z\right],

(108)

where
{}_{p}\Theta_{q}^{(1)}
is a type of hypergeometric function of two variables defined as below.

\displaystyle{}_{p}\Theta_{q}^{(1)}\left[\begin{matrix}\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\\
\gamma_{1}\end{matrix}\middle|\begin{matrix}\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\cdots,\beta_{p+1}\\
\delta_{1},\delta_{2},\cdots,\delta_{q+1}\end{matrix}\middle|z_{1},z_{2}\right]

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\sum_{m_{1},m_{2}=0}^{\infty}\displaystyle\frac{\,{(\alpha_{1})_{m_{1}}(\alpha_{2})_{m_{2}}(\beta_{1})_{m_{1}}}\,}{(\gamma_{1})_{m_{1}}}\displaystyle\frac{\,{(\beta_{2})_{m_{1}+m_{2}}\cdots(\beta_{p+1})_{m_{1}+m_{2}}}\,}{(\delta_{1})_{m_{1}}\cdots(\delta_{q+1})_{m_{1}+m_{2}}}\displaystyle\frac{\,{z_{1}^{m_{1}}z_{2}^{m_{2}}}\,}{m_{1}!m_{2}!}.

(109)

Many relations and theorems regarding hypergeometric functions are employed in this work, and we introduce them when they are needed.

Appendix B
More on the Spectral Function in dS

The spectral function
\rho_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{\text{dS}}(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{\prime})
is of central importance in our bootstrap program for 1-loop diagrams. In this appendix, we provide more discussions about the spectral function in dS. First, for completeness, we reproduce the derivation of the bubble function
B^{\text{EdS}}
